  • Disaggregated prefill/decode coordination: at high replica counts, matching prefill→decode pairs becomes O(prefill_replicas × decode_replicas); use greedy nearest-queue match, not global optimal.

Storage and Metadata Scaling

  • PrefixTree entries: each entry = prefix bytes + BackendId set + timestamp; cap at 100k entries per model, LRU eviction.
  • At 1k models × 100k entries × 64 B avg prefix = ~6.4 GB RAM; impractical → reduce to 10k entries per model = 640 MB; tune per deployment.
  • infr-registry replica metadata: O(replicas × adapters); at 10k replicas × 100 adapters = 1M entries; serialized to ~100 MB; acceptable in-memory.
  • Telemetry cardinality: per-route label set (model_id, adapter_id, backend_id, route_reason) → at 1k models × 10 adapters × 500 backends = 5M label combinations; prohibitive for Prometheus. Mitigation: aggregate at router level (per-model, not per-backend), expose per-backend only on debug endpoint.
  • Lease table: one entry per inflight request; at 100k inflight × 256 B = 25.6 MB; trivially bounded.

Scaling Ceilings Summary

Axis Practical ceiling Hard ceiling Scaling path
Backends per router 500 5k (probe load) horizontal routers + pull scrape
Models tracked 1k 10k (sched saturation) hierarchical scheduler
Inflight requests 100k ~500k (RAM for Leases) horizontal routers
Req/s per instance 50k ~200k (NIC-bound) horizontal + 100 GbE
PrefixTree entries 10k/model 100k/model (RAM) LRU cap + model sharding
Router instances 100 unbounded (stateless) L4 LB capacity

Mental Models & First Principles

MM1 — The Router Is a Lease Manager, Not a Proxy Compressed insight: Every in-flight request is a lease on one backend slot. The router's core invariant is not "forward packets" but "every lease is eventually released exactly once." Prediction: any latency spike that cannot be explained by network or GPU time is a lease acquisition bottleneck — measure lease_acquire_latency_us, not just end-to-end P99. Bug class: Lease leak (I5 violation) manifests as monotonically growing inflight_gauge diverging from actual backend load; backend appears overloaded while queue is empty. Resolving §: §3.7 infr-dispatch (Lease), §7 Invariants (I5), Phase 3.

MM2 — KV-Cache Hit Is a Routing Constraint, Not an Optimization Compressed insight: When prefix-cache hit rate drops below 50%, the router degrades to a load-balancer — this is a qualitative regime change, not a gradual degradation. The PrefixTree is the tightest feedback loop between prompt structure and infrastructure cost. Prediction: a workload with high prefix diversity (random prompts, no system-prompt sharing) will show near-zero KV hit rate and make all PrefixTree logic irrelevant; detect by measuring prefix_match_depth_p50 == 0. Bug class: Eviction storm — when backends are ejected, their PrefixTree entries become stale, causing cold cache routing until the tree is rebuilt; the 5xx storm from ejection can trigger more evictions. Resolving §: Phase 5, §8.5 PrefixTree model, I6, §Scalability Analysis.

MM3 — Admission Control Is the System's Pressure Regulator Compressed insight: A queue with no admission ceiling is an amplifier, not a buffer. Under overload, every request that enters the queue adds latency to all subsequent requests; the correct response is to shed at the boundary (429) and let callers retry with backoff. Prediction: removing the queue_max limit will cause mean latency to grow faster than throughput — the queue depth grows unboundedly while P99 collapses. Bug class: SLO-unaware queuing — admitting a request whose remaining SLO deadline is shorter than the current queue wait (I8 violation) wastes a backend slot that could serve a newer request. Resolving §: Phase 6, §3.3 infr-queue, I4, I8, P1, P6.

MM4 — Circuit Breakers Are State Machines, Not Thresholds Compressed insight: A circuit breaker that only checks "error rate > X" in the last N requests is a reactive threshold. The FSM (Closed → Open → HalfOpen → Closed/Open) provides memory: it prevents a flapping backend from generating repeated error storms during HalfOpen probing. Prediction: a backend that intermittently recovers will cause threshold-only breakers to oscillate; the HalfOpen probe-single-request gate prevents this. Bug class: HalfOpen deadlock — if the probe request itself times out (no response), the breaker must transition back to Open, not stay HalfOpen indefinitely; timer-driven HalfOpen expiry required. Resolving §: Phase 8, §7.10 circuit-breaker FSM, ADR-04 (Envoy outlier detection reference), R4.

MM5 — Hedging Is an At-Most-Once Constraint, Not a Retry Compressed insight: Hedging dispatches a second request speculatively before the first times out; this is fundamentally different from retry (which fires after failure). The at-most-once invariant (I3) means hedging is only legal for idempotent: true requests. The hedge budget (P2) constrains how many requests can have a simultaneous second dispatch. Prediction: mis-applying hedging to non-idempotent requests (e.g., stateful generation with tool calls) causes double-execution; guarded by idempotent: bool check at HedgePolicy gate. Bug class: Double-billing — a hedged request that both replicas complete causes two inference costs but one API charge; cancel the slow replica's connection immediately on first response. Resolving §: Phase 8 §20.2 HedgePolicy, P2 (at-most-once), I3, ADR-15.

MM6 — The Router Must Never Directly Scale Compressed insight: The router emits autoscaling signals (queue depth, TTFT P99, KV utilization) but must never call Kubernetes APIs directly. This preserves separation of concerns: the router observes, the autoscaler decides, the scheduler executes. Prediction: a router that directly patches HPA replicas will race with the Kubernetes control plane under topology changes, causing oscillation. Bug class: Signal amplification — emitting autoscale signals at per-request granularity (rather than per-model debounced) floods the autoscaler with redundant events; debounce to 1 Hz per model. Resolving §: Phase 11 §23.2 batch_hint.rs, P13 (autoscaling signal design), ADR-23, §Operational Readiness.

MM7 — Streaming Relay Correctness Is Flow-Control, Not Forwarding Compressed insight: Token streaming relay is not memcpy — it must respect H2 flow-control windows (I10), release capacity after each DATA frame, and handle mid-stream backend failure by either closing the stream or inserting an error frame. Prediction: a relay that buffers entire responses before forwarding converts streaming latency into batch latency; TTFT collapses to total-generation-time. Bug class: H2 flow-control deadlock — if the router does not release the H2 receive window after forwarding each DATA frame, the backend stalls waiting for window credit while the client is waiting for tokens. Resolving §: Phase 7, §3.7 infr-dispatch streaming relay, I10, B7 (Computer Networking flow-control).

MM8 — Wait-Free Reads Require Epoch-Based Snapshot Discipline Compressed insight: ArcSwap enables wait-free reads on the routing table (registry snapshot) by having writers publish a new Arc without blocking readers holding the old one. But epoch discipline is required: a writer must not free old state until all readers that could be holding it have advanced their epoch. Prediction: using ArcSwap correctly means routing decisions never block on registry updates — measure registry_lock_contention_us should be zero in steady state. Bug class: Torn read — if the registry is updated in two steps (replica added, then adapter map updated) without atomic snapshot replacement, a reader can see a replica without its adapter inventory, causing dispatch to a backend that doesn't serve the requested adapter. Resolving §: Phase 2, §3.5 infr-registry, I1 (single-writer registry), ADR-14.

Comparative Architecture Table

Dimension This Design (infr-router) vLLM Production-Stack / AIBrix NVIDIA Triton Inference Server Ray Serve HuggingFace TGI
Implementation language Rust (zero-GC, MSRV 1.78) Python (asyncio) C++ / Python hybrid Python (asyncio + Ray actors) Rust (router) + Python (model server)
KV-cache-aware routing PrefixTree (radix tree, Blake3/SHA-256 prefix hashes, longest-match) prefix_cache_routing.py (token-hash radix tree, same concept) Not natively; sequence batcher by request ID, not KV content Not natively; queue-aware routing without prefix affinity Partial; prompt caching via backend, not router-level routing
Admission control Bounded mpsc queue + SLO-deadline admission gate; P8 (SLO deadlines govern dispatch); 429 shed Basic queue depth check; no SLO-deadline awareness Dynamic batcher with max_queue_delay; request timeout only Queue per actor replica; backpressure via Ray's object store Token budget admission; no SLO-deadline-aware gate
Circuit breaker Three-state FSM (Closed/Open/HalfOpen) per BackendId; Envoy outlier detection design (R4) None in base router; added at Envoy/Istio sidecar layer None; failed requests returned as error Ray actor restart; no circuit breaker at router layer None; relies on K8s liveness probe
Disaggregated prefill/decode Phase 10 DistServe/Splitwise coordination; PrefillBackend → DecodeBackend KV handoff ≤10ms (§22.4) Experimental; production-stack does not enforce prefill/decode split at router level Not applicable; Triton handles batching internally Not natively; research prototypes only Not supported; single-replica handles full request
Streaming relay H2 flow-control-aware DATA frame relay; SSE/gRPC bidirectional; infr-dispatch; I10 order invariant asyncio streaming relay; H2 via Python aiohttp; no explicit flow-control release guard gRPC bidirectional streaming; internal; not router-level relay HTTP SSE streaming via asyncio; Ray object store for large tensors SSE/gRPC relay; Rust hyper-based for high throughput
Hedging HedgePolicy gated on idempotent: bool; cancel slow replica on first response; P8 (Tail at Scale) None None None None
Autoscaling Emits signals to HPA (queue depth, TTFT P99, KV utilization); never direct K8s API call; ADR-23 Custom metrics via Prometheus; HPA-compatible Triton model analyzer; K8s custom metrics; direct HPA Ray Autoscaler (native); not K8s HPA K8s HPA via standard metrics; no custom signal protocol
Consistency model Eventually consistent PrefixTree (per-instance, rebuilt from health probes); registry single-writer ArcSwap (MM8) Eventually consistent per-instance prefix cache; no cross-instance sync Stateless model repository; model load is durable; no routing consistency concern Actor state is per-replica; no cross-replica KV coordination Stateless at router level; backend manages KV

vLLM production-stack (AIBrix router): The closest architectural antecedent. The prefix_cache_routing.py module uses the same radix tree + token-hash approach as this design's PrefixTree (P3 / SGLang). The key difference is Python's GIL and asyncio event loop: under high RPS (>10k req/s per instance), asyncio throughput saturates while Rust's tokio multi-thread scheduler continues to scale. The vLLM router has no circuit breaker, no SLO-deadline admission gate, and no hedging — these gaps make it unsuitable as-is for latency-sensitive production workloads without a sidecar proxy (Envoy) to fill the resilience gap. This design internalizes those concerns in infr-queue and infr-dispatch.

NVIDIA Triton Inference Server: Triton's dynamic batcher operates inside the model server, not at a routing layer. It groups requests into batches by model_id and max_queue_delay, targeting GPU utilization rather than routing cache affinity. There is no PrefixTree because Triton does not expose KV-cache state to a router. Triton wins in heterogeneous model pipelines (ensemble models, multi-GPU, ONNX/TRT) where Triton's model management and backend abstraction layer outweigh routing-level concerns. The infr-router design would sit in front of a Triton fleet as an admission/routing layer, not replace it.

Ray Serve: Ray Serve's router dispatches requests to actor replicas via Ray's distributed object store. The replica_scheduler.py uses push-based load state (long-poll) rather than pull-based health probes, which enables lower routing latency for load state updates. However, Ray actor overhead (object store serialization, GIL on scheduler) becomes a bottleneck above ~50k req/s. Ray Serve's autoscaling is native to Ray (Ray Autoscaler), not K8s HPA — a mismatch for K8s-only deployments. The infr-router design emits HPA-compatible signals precisely to avoid this dependency.

HuggingFace TGI: TGI's router (router/src/) is written in Rust (matching this design's language choice) and handles SSE streaming relay via hyper. The key difference is that TGI's router is tightly coupled to TGI's backend API (a specific gRPC proto for text generation) while infr-router is backend-agnostic (any OpenAI-compatible or custom gRPC backend). TGI does not implement prefix-cache-aware routing, circuit breakers, or SLO-deadline admission at the router layer. It is an excellent reference for Rust + hyper streaming relay patterns (R4 equivalent for Rust) but is not architected for multi-backend, multi-tenant serving.

Back-of-Envelope Capacity Model

All numbers derived from §4 System Constraints. Formulas shown; verify against actual hardware profiling.

Input constraints (§4):

  • Target: 50,000 req/s per router instance
  • Max inflight: 100,000 (I4, I5 bounded)
  • Backends: up to 500 per instance (Scalability Analysis practical ceiling)
  • Avg prompt prefix: 2,048 tokens × 4 bytes/token = 8,192 bytes
  • Avg output tokens: 256 tokens (streaming, one token = 4 bytes)
  • NIC: 10 GbE = 1.25 GB/s (upgrade path: 100 GbE)

Calculation 1 — Router NIC bandwidth at 50k req/s

Output stream rate = 50,000 req/s × 256 tokens × 4 B/token
                   = 50,000 × 1,024 B
                   = 51,200,000 B/s ≈ 50 MB/s outbound
Input payload rate = 50,000 req/s × 8,192 B/req
                   = 409,600,000 B/s ≈ 400 MB/s inbound
Total NIC demand   = 450 MB/s << 1,250 MB/s (10 GbE)

10 GbE is sufficient at 50k req/s. NIC becomes the bottleneck at ~140k req/s (400MB×140k/50k = 1.12 GB/s). Upgrade to 100 GbE before horizontal scale is exhausted.

Calculation 2 — Router CPU cycles per request (routing hot path)

PrefixTree lookup:      ~500 ns  (ArcSwap read + radix walk 8 nodes)
Policy evaluation:      ~200 ns  (RouteReason enum + SLO check)
Lease acquire:          ~100 ns  (AtomicU64 fetch_add)
Queue enqueue:          ~150 ns  (mpsc send)
Total per-request:      ~950 ns ≈ 1 µs

At 50k req/s: 50,000 × 1 µs = 50 ms/s of CPU
Per core (3 GHz, 1 IPC): 1 core can do 3×10^9 / 950 ≈ 3.1M lookups/s
Cores needed for routing: 50,000 / 3,100,000 = 0.016 cores

Routing hot path is not CPU-bound; tokio I/O wait dominates. 4 worker threads saturate at >>50k req/s routing.

Calculation 3 — Lease table memory at 100k inflight

Lease struct = RequestId(16B) + BackendId(4B) + deadline(8B) + padding(4B) = 32 B
HashMap overhead: load factor 0.75 → capacity = 100,000 / 0.75 ≈ 133,333 slots
Slot size: 32B payload + 8B key pointer + 8B value pointer = 48B
Total: 133,333 × 48B = 6.4 MB

Lease table at max inflight = 6.4 MB. Negligible. Even at 1M inflight: 64 MB.

Calculation 4 — PrefixTree memory at 10k entries/model × 100 models

Per entry: prefix_hash(16B) + BackendId set (avg 3 IDs × 4B) + timestamp(8B) = 36B
Per model: 10,000 entries × 36B = 360 KB
At 100 active models: 100 × 360 KB = 36 MB
HashMap index overhead (× 2): 72 MB

PrefixTree for 100 models fits in 72 MB. At 1,000 models: 720 MB — set max_models_in_tree = 200 and evict cold models.

Calculation 5 — Health probe bandwidth to backend fleet

Probe payload: 512 B (JSON metrics response, gzip)
Probe rate: 500 backends × 1 Hz = 500 probes/s
Probe bandwidth: 500 × 512 B = 256 KB/s inbound (negligible)
At 20 router instances: 20 × 500 = 10,000 probes/s per backend fleet
Backend probe load: 10,000 req/s of /metrics — non-trivial for small replicas

Mitigation: use Prometheus pull model (backends expose /metrics, Prometheus scrapes, routers read Prometheus API) — reduces to 500 req/s to Prometheus.

Calculation 6 — Autoscale signal emission rate

Signal rate: 1,000 models × debounce 1 Hz = 1,000 signals/s
Signal payload: 64 B (model_id + queue_depth + ttft_p99 + kv_util)
Total: 1,000 × 64 B/s = 64 KB/s — negligible

Calculation 7 — Scheduler fairness loop overhead

Per-model scan: 1,000 models × 500 replicas × 10 ns comparison = 5 ms per epoch
Epoch: 100 ms
Scheduler CPU: 5ms / 100ms = 5% of one core

At 10k models: 50 ms per epoch → exceeds 100 ms epoch → scheduler bottleneck. Mitigation: hierarchical scheduler (tenant→model tree, skip inactive tenants).

Calculation 8 — Streaming relay buffer memory

Active streams: 100k inflight × 10% streaming = 10,000 active SSE streams
Buffer per stream: 1 token × 4B = 4B (relay zero-copy, no accumulation)
Buffer total: 10,000 × 4 B = 40 KB (relay) + 64 KB (H2 flow-control window) × 10,000 = 640 MB H2 windows

H2 receive windows dominate at scale. Cap per-stream H2 window to 16 KB: 10,000 × 16 KB = 160 MB. Acceptable.

Hard Questions (Self-Examination)

HQ1 — When does increasing max_queue_depth make P99 latency worse, not better? When queue depth exceeds the product of backend throughput and SLO deadline, admitted requests at the back of the queue will always miss their SLO. A deeper queue does not add capacity — it adds latency for requests that will be shed at dispatch anyway (I8 violation). The correct lever is admission control at queue entry (§3.3 infr-queue, Phase 6), not queue depth. Synthesis: §4 System Constraints (SLO bounds) × Failure Modes & Recovery (queue saturation failure class) × Phase 6.

HQ2 — A backend's kv_util probe reads 0.20 (20%) occupied, but P99 TTFT is spiking to 5s. What are the three most likely causes and which infr- crate owns each?* (1) Probe staleness: health probe interval is 5s, backend loaded up between probes; infr-health (Phase 3). (2) KV-cache fragmentation: 20% utilization is global but contiguous blocks for the request's sequence length are unavailable; not detectable by the scalar probe — requires per-sequence block-map exposure from the backend (future work, §Deferred Features Register). (3) Adapter mismatch: request is routed to a backend that is loading the adapter on-demand (cold LoRA load), blocking prefill; infr-registry adapter inventory (Phase 9). Synthesis: §3.5 infr-health × §3.3 infr-registry × Failure Modes & Recovery TTFT spike.

HQ3 — Explain why the PrefixTree must be rebuilt from health probes rather than maintained as durable state. The PrefixTree maps prefix_hash → BackendId set, representing which backends have that prefix hot in their KV cache. KV caches are volatile (evicted on memory pressure, lost on backend restart). A durable PrefixTree would route to a backend whose KV cache no longer holds the prefix, causing a cold-start penalty indistinguishable from a cache miss. The PrefixTree is soft state: if it disagrees with backend reality, the router makes a suboptimal routing decision (not a correctness failure, I6). Rebuilding from health probes (which report kv_util and active prefixes) keeps the tree consistent with actual cache state. Synthesis: I6 (PrefixTree consistency) × §8.5 PrefixTree model × §Scalability Analysis horizontal scaling.

HQ4 — The circuit breaker for BackendId B is in Open state. A new request arrives for a model only served by B. What happens at each layer of the stack? (1) infr-route policy chain: B is filtered from candidates (circuit breaker Open); no candidates remain. (2) infr-queue: request is held if queue not full, awaiting candidate availability. (3) infr-sched: if no HalfOpen probe has been sent in base_ejection_time, schedules a probe for the next eligible request. (4) infr-dispatch: if probe succeeds → Closed; if fails → extends Open with base_ejection_time × ejection_count. (5) If no candidates available and queue deadline exceeded: 503 returned + autoscale signal emitted. Synthesis: Phase 8 circuit-breaker FSM × Phase 6 admission × Phase 11 autoscale signal × Failure Modes (all-backends-ejected failure class).

HQ5 — How does the consistent hashing ring for session affinity interact with the PrefixTree, and which wins when they conflict? Consistent hashing maps prefix_hash → BackendId for session affinity (same prefix → same backend → KV cache hit). The PrefixTree does a longest-match lookup to find which backend has the deepest cached prefix. When they agree, routing is optimal. When they conflict (consistent hash says B1, PrefixTree says B2 has deeper prefix match), the policy is: PrefixTree wins if match_depth > affinity_threshold (configurable). Rationale: a deeper KV-cache hit reduces GPU compute more than affinity preserves — the marginal cost of breaking affinity is a cold miss on a shorter prefix. Synthesis: Phase 4 §16.2 AffinityTable × Phase 5 PrefixTree design × ADR-08 (ULID) × ADR-16 (PrefixHash).

HQ6 — Under what conditions does hedging increase total backend load rather than decrease user-visible P99? Hedging sends a second request speculatively; if both complete, one is wasted. At high backend utilization (kv_util > 0.80), the second dispatch occupies a KV-cache block that could serve a new request, causing higher queue wait for subsequent requests. The HedgePolicy must gate on: idempotent: true AND backend_utilization < hedge_util_threshold (default 0.60). Above the threshold, hedging trades global throughput for individual P99 — a losing trade under overload. Synthesis: Phase 8 §20.2 HedgePolicy × P8 (Tail at Scale) × §4 System Constraints (kv_util targets) × Failure Modes & Recovery.

HQ7 — Why must the Lease watchdog fire on deadline + grace, not just deadline? The lease deadline is the SLO deadline for the request. If the backend is still generating tokens at the deadline, the response is degraded but may still be useful (partial streaming). The grace period allows in-flight streaming to complete delivery before the lease is force-released. Force-releasing at exactly deadline would cancel an in-flight response at the exact moment the first token might arrive. The grace period must be bounded (e.g., 2× estimated TBT) to prevent lease leaks when backends hang silently. Synthesis: I5 (Lease invariant) × §3.7 infr-dispatch × Phase 7 streaming relay × MM1.

HQ8 — A tenant's quota is exhausted mid-stream. What happens to their active streams? The quota gate (infr-sched quota.rs) applies at admission (queue entry). Active streams that have already passed admission are not cancelled mid-stream — doing so would violate I10 (stream order invariant) and produce a corrupted partial response. The correct behavior: active streams complete; quota enforcement prevents new requests from being admitted. This means a tenant can temporarily exceed quota if many requests entered before quota exhaustion. Mitigation: quota accounting should track admitted-but-incomplete requests as consumed quota, not just completed ones. Synthesis: Phase 11 §23.2 quota.rs × I10 (stream order) × Phase 6 admission × MM3.

HQ9 — If the infr-sched fairness loop runs at 100ms epochs and a high-priority request arrives 99ms into an epoch, what is the maximum admission delay? Up to 100ms (one full epoch) in the worst case, plus queue service time. For latency-sensitive requests (e.g., interactive chat with 200ms TTFT SLO), a 100ms scheduling epoch is 50% of the SLO budget. Mitigation: priority-bypass path — requests with Priority::Critical skip the fairness epoch and are admitted directly to the per-model priority queue without waiting for the next epoch. Synthesis: Phase 11 §23.2 fairness.rs × I8 (SLO deadline admission) × §4 System Constraints (TTFT ≤200ms) × §Scalability Analysis scheduler saturation.

HQ10 — How does the registry single-writer invariant (I1) prevent a torn read, and what is the consequence of violating it? I1 mandates that only infr-registry's writer task may mutate the registry state. The reader sees a complete Arc snapshot via ArcSwap — either the old state or the new state, never a partially-updated state. If two writers race (violating I1), they could produce an intermediate state where a replica is present in the BackendId list but its AdapterInventory has not yet been updated. A router reading this intermediate state routes an adapter request to a backend that doesn't serve the adapter, causing a 4xx or silent wrong-model response. Synthesis: I1 × MM8 × Phase 2, §3.5 infr-registry × ADR-14 (RwLock choice).

HQ11 — Describe the sequence of events that leads from a KV-cache eviction storm on backend B to a 503 cascade, naming each infr- crate involved.* (1) B's KV cache is under memory pressure; evicts cached prefixes to free blocks. (2) infr-health (Phase 3) probes B; kv_util returns >0.95 → Health::Degraded. (3) infr-registry updates B's LoadState; PrefixTree removes B's entries (Phase 5). (4) infr-route redirects all prefix-matched requests to other backends. (5) Those backends now receive elevated load; their KV caches also begin evicting. (6) A cascading health degradation across the fleet; infr-health marks all as Degraded. (7) infr-route finds no Healthy candidates; returns 503. (8) infr-sched emits autoscale signal (scale out). (9) New backends take 5–10 minutes to warm up; cascade persists until warm. Synthesis: Failure Modes & Recovery (KV eviction storm) × Phase 3 × Phase 5 × Phase 8 × Phase 11 × MM2.

HQ12 — What is the correct behavior when the disaggregated prefill backend completes but the decode backend is unavailable for KV handoff? The prefill result (KV blocks) are generated but cannot be transferred to a decode backend. Options: (1) Hold the KV blocks on the prefill backend and retry decode assignment (bounded by SLO deadline); (2) Fall back to unified prefill+decode on the prefill backend if it supports it; (3) Fail the request with 503 and release KV blocks. The correct choice per §22.4: retry decode assignment for up to kv_handoff_timeout (10ms); if no decode backend available, fall back to unified mode on the prefill backend. Synthesis: Phase 10 §22.2 KV handoff × §22.4 acceptance criteria × P5 (Splitwise) × Failure Modes & Recovery disaggregated coordination failure.

HQ13 — Why does telemetry cardinality at (model_id, adapter_id, backend_id, route_reason) explode and what is the principled mitigation? At 1k models × 10 adapters × 500 backends × 6 route_reason variants = 30M label combinations. Prometheus TSDB stores one time series per label set; 30M active series would consume ~30 GB of RAM in the scraper. Principled mitigation: aggregate at the router level. Expose per-model and per-adapter metrics (not per-backend) on the /metrics endpoint. Expose per-backend metrics only on a debug /metrics/debug endpoint not scraped by production Prometheus. Retain route_reason as a label on the router-level series (1k × 10 × 6 = 60k series — manageable). Synthesis: §Scalability Analysis (telemetry cardinality) × Phase 13 §25.4 telemetry × §4 System Constraints × P9 (FIFO Queues cardinality note).

HQ14 — Under a network partition where the router cannot reach any health probes for 30 seconds, what routing decisions does the system make and why? infr-health stops receiving probe responses; it does not immediately mark backends Unhealthy — it applies a health-probe timeout threshold (e.g., 3 consecutive missed probes = Degraded, 5 = Unknown). During the partition, the router continues routing based on stale health state (last known LoadState). Backends in Closed circuit breaker state receive traffic; if they are genuinely down, dispatch will fail (timeout → circuit breaker transition to Open). After partition heals, health probes recover and state is updated. The key invariant: stale health state causes suboptimal routing, not correctness failure (I6 analogy for health). Synthesis: Phase 3 §3.5 infr-health × Phase 8 circuit-breaker FSM × Failure Modes & Recovery health probe partition × MM4.

HQ15 — How does the EWMA smoothing factor for load state affect routing stability versus responsiveness? EWMA: load_ema = α × current_load + (1-α) × load_ema. High α (→1.0): responsive to spikes but noisy — a single bursty second causes routing changes that may reverse immediately, producing oscillation. Low α (→0.0): smooth but slow — a backend that becomes overloaded is still sent traffic for many seconds while EMA catches up. The correct α depends on the probe interval: for 1 Hz probes, α=0.3 gives a ~3-probe settling time (3s responsiveness). For 10 Hz probes, α=0.1 gives the same settling time. The wrong α causes either thundering-herd (high α, many routers simultaneously route away from a backend) or delayed-overload (low α). Synthesis: Phase 2, §3.5 infr-registry LoadState × §8.5 PrefixTree model × §Scalability Analysis replication pressure.

HQ16 — What prevents the admit-from-queue fairness loop from starving low-priority requests indefinitely? The fairness loop (infr-sched fairness.rs) uses weighted fair queuing: each priority tier receives a quota of admits per epoch proportional to its weight, not a strict priority ordering. A Critical request does not prevent a Normal request from being admitted if the Critical quota for this epoch is already filled. Additionally, requests carry a maximum queue age (derived from SLO deadline): if a Normal request has been queued beyond its age limit, it is shed (429) rather than starved indefinitely, which prevents unbounded queue age growth. Synthesis: Phase 11 §23.2 fairness.rs × Phase 6 §3.3 infr-queue shed policy × I4 (bounded-queue) × I8 (SLO deadline admission).

HQ17 — Name three observable signals that distinguish "router is overloaded" from "backend fleet is overloaded." (1) router_queue_depth_p99 > 0 and backend_kv_util_p50 < 0.50: router is queuing requests while backends are underutilized — router admission or routing logic is the bottleneck, not backend capacity. (2) lease_acquire_latency_us_p99 > 1000 with low backend latency: Lease contention inside infr-dispatch is the bottleneck (router-side). (3) dispatch_retry_rate > 5% with all backends Healthy: router is dispatching and backends are rejecting (429/503) — backend admission is the bottleneck, not router. Synthesis: Phase 13 §25.4 telemetry × §Performance Engineering (M1–M10) × Failure Modes & Recovery × MM1.

Alternative Architectures (Roads Not Taken)

AA1 — Sidecar Proxy Model (Envoy + xDS control plane)

  Client
    │
    ▼
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  Ingress LB (L7, TLS termination)                   │
└─────────────────┬───────────────────────────────────┘
                  │
      ┌───────────┴───────────┐
      ▼                       ▼
 ┌─────────┐             ┌─────────┐
 │  Envoy  │             │  Envoy  │  (per-pod sidecar)
 │ sidecar │             │ sidecar │
 └────┬────┘             └────┬────┘
      │  xDS config            │
      └───────────┬────────────┘
                  ▼
         ┌─────────────┐
         │  xDS Control│
         │    Plane    │  (Istio / custom)
         └──────┬──────┘
                │
       ┌────────┴────────┐
       ▼                 ▼
 ┌──────────┐      ┌──────────┐
 │  vLLM    │      │  vLLM    │   (backends)
 │ replica  │      │ replica  │
 └──────────┘      └──────────┘

Regime where this wins: heterogeneous backend fleet (multiple model servers: Triton, vLLM, TGI) where a unified L7 proxy policy layer is already mandated by the platform team, and per-sidecar circuit breaking + mTLS is required by a zero-trust security posture. Real system: Istio + Envoy. Why rejected for this design: Envoy has no LLM-semantic routing (KV-cache prefix affinity, SLO-deadline admission, token-budget-aware scheduling) — these require custom Lua/WASM filters or an out-of-band control plane. The operational complexity of running xDS is higher than a purpose-built router. The sidecar model introduces a hop per request, doubling streaming relay latency.

AA2 — Centralized Scheduler with Worker Pull (Orca/vLLM iteration-level)

  Client → REST/gRPC
    │
    ▼
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│  Centralized Scheduler (single process)             │
│  - Global batch window (iteration-level scheduling) │
│  - All replicas pull next batch from scheduler      │
│  - Token budget tracking across all replicas        │
└──────────────────────────┬──────────────────────────┘
                           │  pull
              ┌────────────┼────────────┐
              ▼            ▼            ▼
         ┌────────┐   ┌────────┐   ┌────────┐
         │ Worker │   │ Worker │   │ Worker │
         │ GPU    │   │ GPU    │   │ GPU    │
         └────────┘   └────────┘   └────────┘

Regime where this wins: homogeneous GPU fleet, all replicas running the same model, and iteration-level continuous batching is the primary optimization target (maximizing MFU). The scheduler has global visibility of all in-flight sequences and can make globally-optimal batch decisions. Real system: vLLM's internal scheduler (within a single vLLM instance), Orca (P1). Why rejected: a centralized scheduler is a single point of failure and a serialization bottleneck for the entire fleet. At 500 backends, the scheduler's O(sequences × replicas) matching loop becomes the bottleneck. This design uses a distributed router (each router instance operates independently) and delegates iteration-level scheduling to individual backends — the router makes routing decisions, not batching decisions.

AA3 — Gossip-Based Distributed KV-Cache Index

  ┌──────────┐    gossip     ┌──────────┐
  │ Router 1 │◄─────────────►│ Router 2 │
  │PrefixTree│               │PrefixTree│
  └────┬─────┘               └────┬─────┘
       │                          │
       ▼                          ▼
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Shared KV-Cache Index (gossip)     │
  │  prefix_hash → {BackendId, version} │
  └───────────────┬─────────────────────┘
                  │
       ┌──────────┼──────────┐
       ▼          ▼          ▼
  ┌────────┐ ┌────────┐ ┌────────┐
  │Backend │ │Backend │ │Backend │
  └────────┘ └────────┘ └────────┘

Regime where this wins: large multi-router deployments (>50 router instances) where per-instance PrefixTree divergence causes significant cross-instance cache miss penalty. A gossip-based distributed index allows all routers to share prefix occupancy information with eventual consistency (similar to how Maglev handles consistent hashing across nodes). Real system: Mooncake (P6) uses a distributed KV transfer store with a global index. Why rejected (current phase): gossip protocol adds complexity (vector clocks, anti-entropy, partition handling), increases memory per router (full fleet PrefixTree), and the current design's session affinity via consistent hashing (ADR-08) already reduces cross-instance cache misses when L4 LB uses prefix_hash-based ECMP. Gossip is listed in Deferred Features Register for >50-instance deployments.

Deferred Features Register

# Feature Deferred From Reason Revisit Trigger
D1 Gossip-based cross-router PrefixTree sync §8.5, Phase 5 Adds gossip protocol complexity; session affinity mitigates at <50 router instances >50 router instances in production; measured cross-instance cache miss rate >20%
D2 Per-sequence KV-block-map probe (fragmentation detection) §3.5, Phase 3 Requires backend API extension; not yet supported by vLLM/TGI backends Backend exposes /metrics/kv_block_map endpoint; HQ2 failure mode confirmed in production
D3 Global-optimal batch matching for disaggregated prefill/decode §8.9, Phase 10 O(prefill × decode) matching too expensive at scale; greedy nearest-queue used Fleet >1k disaggregated replicas; scheduler epoch exceeds 50ms
D4 Hierarchical tenant→model scheduler tree §8.10, Phase 11 Active-only model optimization deferred; flat scan adequate at <1k models >1k active models in a single router shard; fairness loop epoch >50ms measured
D5 Direct K8s HPA patch (not signal emit) §8.10, ADR-23 Violates separation of concerns; HPA pull from Prometheus adapter is standard Prometheus adapter unavailable in deployment environment; autoscale latency >5 min SLA
D6 WASM plugin filter chain for custom routing policies §25.2, Phase 13 Significant runtime complexity; V8/Wasmtime embedding; not needed for initial policies Enterprise customers requiring custom routing logic without router recompilation
D7 Multi-region KV-cache transfer (cross-datacenter) §8.9, Phase 10 Network RTT (>10ms) violates KV handoff latency target (≤10ms); requires RDMA Multi-region active-active LLM serving with shared model; RDMA available between DCs
D8 Auth/billing enforcement (policy engine, not just the plug-in trait) §1.2 System Boundary (NON-Goals) Explicit non-goal (§1.3); router exposes a plug-in trait boundary only, no enforcement logic A deployment requires in-router auth/billing enforcement rather than an external gateway
D9 Full edge-TLS identity policy engine (SPIFFE/mTLS client-cert chains) §1.2, ADR-24 ADR-24 covers backend-leg mTLS only; a client-identity policy engine at the ingress edge is deferred Zero-trust mesh mandates SPIFFE/SPIRE workload identity at the router's client-facing edge
D10 Cross-router distributed state via Raft consensus §8.5 PrefixTree Model, AA3 AA3 (gossip, D1) already deferred; a stronger Raft-consensus variant deferred further pending a correctness requirement A deployment requires strongly-consistent (not eventually-consistent) cross-router routing state
D11 Speculative-decode draft/verify coordination §8.9 Disaggregated Prefill/Decode Coordination, Phase 10 Goal states routing awareness of speculative decoding; full draft-model/verify-model coordination protocol deferred pending backend API standardization Backend fleet exposes a standard speculative-decode draft-token API
D12 Multi-modal routing (image/audio token prefixes) §1.2 System Boundary PrefixHash/PrefixTree defined over text token sequences only; image/audio tokenization is unstandardized across backends Backend fleet standardizes a multi-modal token representation compatible with PrefixHash
D13 Dynamic LoRA hot-load trigger (on-demand) Phase 9 (§21) Adapter-Aware Routing Adapter inventory refreshed only via the health-probe scrape cycle (Phase 3 cadence); an on-demand hot-load RPC is deferred Backend exposes an on-demand LoRA load endpoint with a bounded latency SLO

Glossary

Term Definition
Admission control Gate before queue entry; rejects or backpressures requests when queue is full or SLO deadline already expired at arrival. Implemented in infr-queue.
AdapterId Opaque newtype (String) identifying a LoRA/PEFT fine-tune adapter loaded onto one or more replicas. Distinct from ModelId (base weights).
At-most-once Dispatch invariant: non-idempotent Request reaches exactly one backend; hedging forbidden unless idempotent: true. Prevents double-charging or duplicate state mutation.
Autoscaling signal Scalar or event emitted by infr-sched to an external HPA/cluster-autoscaler; carries queue depth, TTFT p99, and KV utilization. Does not directly scale; only advises.
BackendId Newtype (u32); stable identifier for a Replica within the registry. Reused on restart if address is same; new id on re-registration after eviction.
Backpressure Flow-control mechanism: when infr-queue is saturated, infr-ingress pauses token reading on the TCP/HTTP/2 stream rather than allocating more memory.
Batching hint Advisory from infr-sched to a backend suggesting it combine N queued requests in one forward pass (continuous batching); backend may ignore.
Bounded-queue invariant All queues in infr-queue have a fixed max capacity; saturation triggers shedding or backpressure, never heap growth. Prevents OOM under spike load.
Circuit breaker State machine in infr-dispatch with states Closed/Open/Half-Open; trips on consecutive errors or latency breach; prevents request storms to a failing backend.
Consistent hashing Hash ring mapping ModelId + session_key -> BackendId; minimizes remapping when replicas are added/removed; one policy in infr-route.
Continuous batching LLM serving technique (Orca, 2022) where the forward pass is executed continuously and new requests join mid-batch at iteration boundaries, maximizing GPU utilization without fixed batch windows.
Decision auditability Every RoutingDecision carries a RouteReason variant; logged to infr-telemetry enabling post-hoc analysis of routing choices per request.
Decode phase Second phase of LLM inference; autoregressive per-token generation using KV cache built in prefill; typically memory-bandwidth-bound, lower compute intensity.
Disaggregation Architecture (DistServe/Splitwise) separating prefill and decode onto different GPU pools; router must coordinate handoff of KV-cache state between prefill-replica and decode-replica.
EWMA load Exponentially-weighted moving average of a metric (e.g., TTFT, queue depth); infr-health computes with configurable half-life; dampens transient spikes in routing decisions.
Ejection Outlier-ejection: replica transitions Health::Ejected after consecutive errors or latency outlier detection; no requests routed to it until recovery timer expires.
Fairness Policy in infr-sched ensuring no tenant/model starves; implemented via weighted fair queuing or token-bucket per tenant.
Graceful drain infr-ingress shutdown mode: stop accepting new connections, wait for inflight requests to complete up to drain timeout, then close; preserves streaming clients.
Hedging Speculative retry: send duplicate request to second backend after hedge delay; only legal when Request.idempotent == true; cancel slower response on first completion.
Health Enum { Healthy, Degraded, Ejected } per Replica; drives routing eligibility. Degraded still routes but with penalty weight; Ejected is fully excluded.
Health invariant Requests are never dispatched to Health::Ejected replicas; enforced in infr-route before issuing RoutingDecision.
Idempotency Request.idempotent: bool; when true, hedging and safe retry allowed; when false, at-most-once enforced. Caller asserts; router does not verify semantics.
InferError Enum of error variants: Timeout, BackendUnavailable, ModelNotLoaded, AdapterNotLoaded, StreamBroken, RateLimited, InternalError, QueueFull.
infr-core Root crate exporting all shared types; no I/O, no async; all other crates depend on it. Zero transitive deps outside std + serde + ulid.
KV cache Key-value attention cache (PagedAttention, vLLM); backends store KV tensors per request; kv_util: f32 in LoadState tracks utilization 0.0–1.0.
Lease { request: RequestId, backend: BackendId, deadline: Instant }; held from routing decision until response complete or timeout; freeing updates backend accounting atomically.
Lease invariant Each admitted request holds exactly one Lease at a time; issuing a second Lease for the same request is a bug; enforced in infr-dispatch.
LoadState Snapshot of backend load: { queue_depth, kv_util, running_batch, ewma_ttft_ms, inflight }; scraped by infr-health and stored in registry.
LoRA adapter Low-Rank Adaptation fine-tune weights layered on top of base model; must be loaded on target replica; AdapterId routes to adapter-resident replicas.
ModelId Newtype (String) identifying base model weights (e.g., "llama3-70b"); foreign key into registry's model->replica map.
Order invariant Byte order of token stream from backend is preserved to client exactly; infr-dispatch relay must not reorder, merge, or buffer chunks out of order.
Outlier ejection Detection algorithm comparing a replica's p99 TTFT to fleet mean; if deviation exceeds threshold, transitions replica to Health::Ejected for a penalty interval.
PagedAttention vLLM memory management (Kwon 2023) dividing KV cache into fixed-size pages; enables fine-grained sharing and eviction; kv_util reflects page pool saturation.
Prefill phase First LLM inference phase: processes all prompt tokens in one (often parallel) forward pass; compute-bound; produces KV cache; TTFT directly depends on it.
Prefix cache / RadixAttention SGLang (Zheng 2024) radix-tree structure reusing KV cache segments for shared prompt prefixes across requests; PrefixTree in infr-route maps prefix hashes to backends holding the cache.
PrefixHash u64 rolling hash of prompt token prefix; computed in infr-ingress; used by infr-route for longest-prefix match against PrefixTree.
PrefixTree Radix tree token-prefix -> Set<BackendId> with recency metadata; maintained by infr-registry updated on dispatch outcomes; drives prefix-cache-aware routing.
Priority Enum { RealTime, High, Normal, Batch }; determines queue ordering and SLO enforcement strictness in infr-queue.
RequestId Newtype (Ulid); monotonic, sortable, globally unique; used as correlation key in logs, traces, and lease tracking.
Replica Active backend instance: { id: BackendId, model: ModelId, adapters: HashSet<AdapterId>, load: LoadState, health: Health, addr: SocketAddr }.
RouteReason Enum { LeastLoad, PrefixHit, AdapterResident, SessionAffinity, ConsistentHash, Fallback, Hedged }; attached to every RoutingDecision for auditability.
RoutingDecision { backend: BackendId, reason: RouteReason }; output of infr-route policy engine; input to infr-dispatch.
Session affinity Routing policy binding a client session key (e.g., X-Session-Id) to a consistent backend for KV cache reuse; degrades gracefully to least-load on backend failure.
Shedding Active request rejection (HTTP 429 / gRPC RESOURCE_EXHAUSTED) when queue is full or deadline already expired; preferred over silent OOM or unbounded latency.
SLO { ttft_ms: u32, tpot_ms: u32, deadline: Instant }; per-request service-level objective; infr-queue ages requests and sheds if deadline expires in queue.
SSE Server-Sent Events; HTTP/1.1 streaming token delivery format; infr-ingress emits data: {token}\n\n chunks from the backend relay.
TTFT Time-To-First-Token: latency from request receipt to first token byte delivered to client; primary LLM latency SLO metric; tracked as EWMA per backend.
TPOT Time-Per-Output-Token: inter-token latency during decode phase; secondary SLO metric; computed per request in infr-telemetry.
Tenant Logical isolation unit for fairness/quota enforcement in infr-sched; identified by header or API key prefix; quota tracked as token-bucket.
vLLM Open-source LLM inference engine (PagedAttention); primary reference backend implementation; exposes /v1/completions HTTP API compatible with OpenAI spec.
Weighted fair queuing Scheduling algorithm in infr-sched allocating dispatch slots proportionally to tenant weights; prevents single tenant monopolizing capacity.

Appendix B — Milestone Summary

Phase Deliverable Duration Key Risks Exit Criteria
0 — Skeleton (§12) 11-crate workspace; infr-core canonical types; single-backend reverse proxy relays one request byte-for-byte 1–2 weeks Core-type churn destabilizing downstream crates; serde/ULID compatibility drift cargo build --workspace zero warnings; RequestId 0 collisions/10k; echo-body SHA-256 match; p99 overhead < 2 ms; RSS < 20 MB; graceful SIGTERM
1 — Ingress (§13) infr-ingress: HTTP/1.1 keep-alive, HTTP/2 mux, gRPC framing, SSE/chunked relay; graceful drain; stubbed RoutingDecision 2 weeks HTTP/2 and gRPC streaming edge cases; drain correctness under in-flight streams All three protocols share one port; Request fully populated; drain completes in-flight before exit; /healthz reports phase
2 — Registry (§14) infr-registry: ReplicaSet, ModelRegistry, AdapterInventory, CapacityDescriptor; TOML config load; SIGHUP hot reload via ArcSwap 1 week Torn reads during hot reload; stale snapshots served to routing GET /debug/registry reflects live state; SIGHUP reload changes replica count with zero in-flight disruption
3 — Health (§15) infr-health: heartbeat probes, LoadState scrape, EWMA load score, Healthy→Degraded→Ejected FSM with recovery 1 week Probe false-positives ejecting healthy replicas; EWMA α mistuned for probe interval No Ejected replica ever appears in a RoutingDecision; EWMA convergence tests pass; GET /admin/health live
4 — Basic Routing Policies (§16) infr-route PolicyEngine: RoundRobin, Weighted, LeastLoad, SessionAffinity; RouteReason on every decision; fallback chain 1 week Fallback chain masking a genuine all-Ejected outage; affinity-TTL expiry storms 10k-request integration test selects zero Ejected backends; RouteReason populated on every decision
5 — Prefix/KV-Cache-Aware Routing (§17) PrefixTree radix structure; longest-prefix-match routing; prefix_hash computed at ingress; post-completion cache registration; eviction tracking 2 weeks PrefixTree write-lock contention under high insert rate; stale entries after silent backend eviction PrefixHit path measurably reduces routed-load skew; GET /admin/prefix-tree reports hot prefixes; concurrent-update tests pass
6 — Admission Control + Queueing (§18) infr-queue bounded per-model priority queues; SLO-deadline aging; shedding; backpressure signal to ingress 1 week Priority inversion under mixed-priority bursts; bounded-queue invariant violated by mis-sized capacity config Bounded invariant holds at 10x capacity spike; HTTP 429 with x-queue-util on shed; backpressure watch verified
7 — Dispatch + Connection Pooling (§19) infr-dispatch multiplexed HTTP/2 pool; streaming relay; TTFT/total timeouts; circuit-breaker stub 2 weeks H2 flow-control window stalls under sustained streaming; connection-pool exhaustion under backend slowdown End-to-end SSE response through full pipeline; > 95% connection reuse; zero window stalls in 10-min load test
8 — Resilience (§20) Retries (idempotent-only) + hedging + full circuit-breaker FSM in infr-dispatch; outlier ejection in infr-health; deadline propagation header 2 weeks Hedge-cancel race duplicating tokens to client; circuit breaker flapping under intermittent recovery At-most-once and order invariants hold under chaos; GET /debug/circuits shows FSM state; 30%-error-injection chaos test shows measurable p99 improvement
9 — Adapter-Aware + Multi-Model Routing (§21) Adapter inventory + swap-cost model in infr-registry; adapter-resident hard filter in infr-route; RouteReason::adapter_resident/adapter_swap 2 weeks Adapter-inventory drift between registry and live backend state; swap-cost mis-weighting causing thrash Zero dispatch to a backend lacking the requested adapter; GET /debug/adapters matches live backend state
10 — Disaggregated Prefill/Decode + Speculative (§22) ReplicaRole tagging; KV-handoff coordination between prefill/decode pools; two-hop speculative-decode dispatch 3 weeks KV-handoff timeout stalling decode; speculative two-hop doubling tail latency under handoff failure Decode never starts before handoff ACK (I12); kv_handoff_latency_ms ≤ 10 ms p99; disaggregation_enabled hot-toggle shifts traffic within one tick
11 — Autoscaling Signals + Fairness (§23) infr-sched ScaleSignal emission; per-tenant WFQ; infr-queue token-bucket rate limiting 2 weeks Low-weight tenant starvation under sustained high-weight saturation; signal flood from non-debounced per-request emission No starvation in 60s mixed-tenant test; ScaleSignal fires at threshold; quotas hot-reload without restart
12 — Observability (§24) infr-telemetry full metrics/tracing/logging pipeline across all crates; Grafana dashboards 1 week Label-cardinality explosion at (model, adapter, backend, reason) granularity; telemetry overhead regressing TTFT ≤ 200 µs p99 telemetry overhead; all 20 metrics present after one request; OTLP spans verified against local collector
13 — Conformance, Load + Chaos, Benchmarks (§25) infr-bench conformance/chaos/differential/throughput/tail-latency harness; CI regression gate 2 weeks Non-hermetic chaos tests flaking in CI; benchmark variance exceeding the ±5% reproducibility bound All conformance fixtures pass; CI blocks merge on > 5% throughput drop or > 10% P99 rise; baseline stored in infr-bench/baseline/results.json
